# Task: SQLCipher Phase 1.6 — Critical Security Hardening

The Phase 1.5 audit identified two HIGH-severity release blockers and several MEDIUM/LOW findings.

Read:

- SQLCIPHER\_PHASE1\_5\_SECURITY\_AUDIT.md
- SQLCIPHER\_PHASE1\_IMPLEMENTATION\_REPORT.md
- SECURITY.md
- DATABASE\_ENCRYPTION.md

## Mission

Fix the critical database availability and downgrade split-brain defects without starting Phase 2 migration.

## Strict constraints

- Do not implement plaintext-to-encrypted migration.
- Do not change database schemas or entities.
- Do not modify E2EE code.
- Do not modify either physical phone.
- Do not clear application data.
- Preserve all existing user data.
- Use synthetic databases and disposable emulators.
- Do not commit.

## F-01: Database unavailable recovery

Implement a proper application-level database availability state.

Requirements:

1. Introduce explicit states:
   - READY
   - INITIALIZING
   - KEY\_MISSING
   - KEY\_INVALIDATED
   - KEY\_CORRUPTED
   - DATABASE\_CORRUPTED
   - DATABASE\_UNAVAILABLE
2. Prevent Compose from accessing repositories before READY.
3. Show a dedicated recovery screen instead of crashing.
4. Never display empty conversations when database access fails.
5. Never generate a replacement key automatically when an encrypted database already exists.
6. Preserve encrypted DB and wrapped-key files during recovery.
7. Provide actionable, non-destructive recovery guidance.
8. Ensure background workers respect the same availability state.
9. Add tests for every state and transition.

Do not add a destructive reset button.

## F-02: Downgrade split-brain protection

Implement a fail-closed database-format guard.

Requirements:

1. Detect simultaneous presence of:
   - `konnex.db`
   - `konnex-encrypted.db`
2. Never silently prefer the plaintext database when an encrypted database exists.
3. Never let an older-build plaintext database shadow encrypted data.
4. Detect legacy database creation after an encrypted installation.
5. Prevent normal application operation in ambiguous database states.
6. Preserve both database files until an explicit recovery procedure is approved.
7. Test:
   - Phase 1 → legacy APK → Phase 1.
   - Legacy APK → Phase 1.
   - Encrypted DB + plaintext shadow.
   - Missing encrypted DB + existing key.
   - Missing key + encrypted DB.
   - Repeated upgrade/downgrade cycles.

If server-side minimum-client enforcement is available, investigate it, but do not change server behavior without separate approval.

## Medium findings

Fix where safe:

- Correct error classification for malformed wrapped-key data.
- Distinguish missing Keystore alias from other Keystore failures.
- Stop swallowing encrypted database errors in SecurityInspector.
- Prevent misleading "Planned" status when encryption is enabled but unavailable.
- Avoid unnecessary immutable raw-key String copies.
- Minimize key lifetime in memory where practical.
- Clean up temporary files after failed fresh database creation without deleting valid keys or databases.

Do not introduce speculative key rotation or recovery mechanisms.

## Low findings

Document and fix only if the change is isolated and testable:

- Key creation race.
- Unused key-file version.
- Global DatabaseAccess state.
- Test artifact cleanup.
- Rename fallback safety.

## Regression requirements

Run:

- Full unit tests.
- Full server tests.
- API 28 instrumentation.
- API 35 instrumentation.
- Debug build.
- Release build.
- Minified release build.
- Upgrade/downgrade matrix.

Verify no plaintext fallback and no data loss.

`SQLCIPHER_PHASE1_6_HARDENING_REPORT.md`

Do not begin Phase 2.

Phase 1.6 hardening is implemented and verified. Report: `SQLCIPHER_PHASE1_6_HARDENING_REPORT.md`.

**F-01 — database availability**
- Added typed `DatabaseState` (`INITIALIZING`, `READY`, `KEY_MISSING`, `KEY_INVALIDATED`, `KEY_CORRUPTED`, `DATABASE_CORRUPTED`, `DATABASE_UNAVAILABLE`, plus `AMBIGUOUS_DATABASES`), `DatabaseAvailability`, and a testable state tracker.
- `AppContainer.initializeDatabase()` never throws; it publishes READY or a typed failure. `MainActivity` gates the entire graph: repositories and `KonnexRoot` are only composed after READY; otherwise a non-destructive recovery screen explains the reason with no reset/delete button.
- Verified on-device: missing key, replaced database, and ambiguous files now keep the process alive and show the recovery screen. Previously all three crashed with `FATAL EXCEPTION: main`.
- Sync/backup workers now consume the same availability through their Application interfaces; the old global `DatabaseAccess` state was removed.

**F-02 — downgrade split-brain**
- `DatabaseBootstrap` now checks both `konnex.db` and `konnex-encrypted.db` first and fails closed with `AMBIGUOUS_DATABASES`, opening/deleting neither.
- Verified: legacy-only → hardened works normally; hardened → legacy creates plaintext; re-upgrade shows "Two local databases found" with both files and the key preserved. Repeated the full cycle twice; deleting the synthetic shadow restores the encrypted data.

**Medium/low fixes**
- Malformed Base64/envelope/version → `KEY_CORRUPTED`; wrong/replaced database → `DATABASE_CORRUPTED`; Keystore alias loss vs provider failure distinguished.
- `SecurityInspector` no longer touches or swallows the database; Settings/Profile show Enabled / Legacy plaintext / Unavailable / Checking instead of "Planned".
- Raw-key literal built as bytes (no immutable hex `String`); raw key zeroed after spec creation.
- Key creation uses a JVM lock + OS file lock + atomic publish; failed fresh creation cleans `-wal`/`-shm`/`-journal`; key-file version validated; test key artifacts cleaned up.
- No key rotation or speculative recovery mechanism added.

**Verification**
- JVM: `./gradlew test --rerun-tasks` **BUILD SUCCESSFUL** — 95 unique cases, 0 failures/skips; 5 new database-state tests.
- Server: 35/35, 0 failures.
- API 35 and API 28 instrumentation: **23/23 OK on each** (17 SQLCipher/security + startup + persistence + outbox).
- Debug, release and R8-minified release builds succeed; minified release installed and launched with its encrypted database.
- Truncated/interrupted update rejected; installed version stayed intact.

**Not changed:** schemas/entities, migration, E2EE, backup/attachment encryption, server behavior, physical phones. Nothing committed.

**Remaining risks:** no recoverable encrypted export (F-03), ambiguous state requires Phase 2/support to resolve, legacy installs remain plaintext until migration, and the SQLCipher factory necessarily retains the key literal for the process lifetime. Phase 2 has not started; awaiting approval.

Continue the KONNEX project.

## 1\. Read the existing documentation first

## 2\. Objective

Implement the actual migration from:

- `konnex.db` — existing plaintext Room database.
- `konnex-encrypted.db` — SQLCipher 4.17.0 encrypted database.

Preserve all existing application data, including:

- Messages
- Conversations
- Profiles
- Contacts
- Outbox
- Synchronization state
- All other related records

Do not modify the database schema unless absolutely necessary.

## 3\. Migration safety requirements

These requirements are mandatory:

- Migration must run before any database consumer accesses the database.
- Account for WorkManager auto-initialization and workers running in separate processes.
- Implement a cross-process file lock.
- Implement a persistent migration state machine.
- Support process death, force-stop, reboot, and insufficient storage.
- Never silently fall back to plaintext if encrypted database initialization fails.
- Never overwrite an existing valid database.
- Checkpoint WAL and verify that the WAL is empty before export.
- Verify `cipher_integrity_check`, `quick_check`, schema integrity, and row counts.
- Retain the plaintext source until the encrypted database has passed verification across two independent cold starts.
- Delete plaintext database, WAL, SHM, and journal files only after successful verification.
- Make cleanup safe against interruptions.
- Never leave plaintext temporary files behind.

## 4\. Dual-database and downgrade protection

Preserve the Phase 1.6 security behavior:

- Legacy-only database: allow migration.
- Encrypted-only database: open the encrypted database.
- Both databases present: fail closed. Never automatically select one.
- Encrypted database corrupted or inaccessible: display the appropriate recovery state. Never create a replacement plaintext database.
- Downgrade followed by upgrade: must never silently restore plaintext database usage.

## 5\. UI and recovery

Integrate with the existing `DatabaseAvailability` architecture.

Provide appropriate UI states for:

- Preparing encrypted database
- Migration in progress
- Migration interrupted
- Insufficient storage
- Migration failed
- Recovery required

The UI must clearly communicate what is happening.

Do not introduce automatic database deletion, destructive reset buttons, or silent data-loss recovery mechanisms.

## 6\. Mandatory testing

Use isolated emulators first. Do not install, modify, reset, or run tests on physical phones without explicit authorization.

Test the following scenarios:

1. Legacy-only database migration.
2. Empty database migration.
3. Large database migration.
4. Process killed at every migration checkpoint.
5. Force-stop and restart.
6. Device reboot during migration.
7. Disk-full conditions.
8. Corrupted plaintext source.
9. Corrupted encrypted temporary database.

11. Incorrect Keystore key.
12. Concurrent migration attempts from two processes.
13. Worker starting while migration is running.
14. Downgrade and subsequent re-upgrade.

16. Plaintext residue inspection after migration.

Run the complete migration test suite on:

- API 28
- API 35

Use synthetic test data. Do not expose real user messages or credentials in test logs.

- JVM unit tests.
- Server tests.

- Release build.

- Existing synchronization tests.

Do not claim a test passed unless it was actually executed successfully.

Do not skip failing tests or conceal regressions.

## 8\. Security constraints

- Never log database keys or plaintext message contents.
- Zeroize sensitive byte arrays wherever technically possible.
- Never store database keys in SharedPreferences.
- Never introduce destructive fallback behavior.
- Do not modify the E2EE protocol.
- Do not modify server-side protocol behavior.
- Do not implement attachment or backup encryption in this milestone.
- Clearly document that backups and attachments remain outside the scope of this database encryption milestone.

The goal is a production-quality, crash-safe database migration that preserves existing user data and never silently compromises encryption.

The architecture is set: a fixed-size, versioned container whose immutable core is authenticated into every chunk, with independently authenticated wrapped DEK slots so passphrase/recovery rotation can rewrite only the header. Automatic backups will use a per-install 160-bit recovery key held encrypted under a dedicated Keystore alias; manual backups add a passphrase as a second independent unlock path, and no passphrase is ever stored. ## Phase 2.2 implemented

Encrypted backups now use a versioned `KONNXBK2` container:

- AES-256-GCM with 64 KiB chunks, unique nonces, authenticated chunk order/final markers, and an encrypted footer binding plaintext length, chunk count and SHA-256.
- One random 32-byte DEK per backup, independently wrapped by an Argon2id passphrase KEK (64 MiB, t=3, p=1) and an HKDF-SHA256 key derived from a 160-bit recovery key.
- The header core is authenticated as AAD in every chunk, footer and wrap slot.
- Passphrase change and recovery rotation rewrite only a 64-byte wrapped-DEK slot; the encrypted body remains byte-identical.
- Restore authenticates the entire container before parsing the ZIP, validates schema/counts/IDs/FKs, and imports in one Room transaction.
- Scheduled backups use a recovery key sealed under Keystore alias `konnex.backup.recovery.v1`; the passphrase is never stored.
- The inner ZIP remains in memory only. It is never written to disk or passed to a storage provider.

I also fixed three review findings after the first green run: restore now wipes its backing buffer directly, repeated rewraps use bounded unique object names, and the Keystore save path wipes sealed bytes on every outcome.

## Phase 2.3 complete — attachment encryption at rest

Implemented end-to-end local attachment encryption, resumable plaintext migration, encrypted-backup integration, a streaming content provider, documentation, and verification. **No commit was made.**

### Encryption architecture

- **Tink streaming AEAD** `AES256_GCM_HKDF_4KB` (AES-256-GCM with HKDF-SHA256 and 4 KiB segments). No custom cipher or framing.
- **One independent random Tink keyset per attachment**; never shared with SQLCipher, backups, other attachments, or tokens.
- Keysets are wrapped with a dedicated non-exportable Keystore key, alias **`konnex.attachment.wrap.v1`**.
- Files: `files/attachments/<id>.konnexatt` plus `<id>.konnexatt.key`.
- **16-byte versioned header** `KONNXAT1` / version 1 / algorithm 1 / flags 0.
- Header plus `(attachmentId, ownerId, formatVersion, mimeType)` is authenticated as Tink AAD. The key sidecar is wrapped with the same metadata under a separate key-AAD tag.
- Incremental 64 KiB processing; temp file → fsync → atomic move. Plaintext attachments are never written to persistent storage.
- `read()` is limited to 32 MiB in memory; larger data uses `openDecrypted()` streaming.
- Legacy plaintext paths are rejected; there is **no plaintext fallback**.
- Read-only, non-exported `AttachmentContentProvider` streams decrypted bytes through a pipe under a per-URI grant.

### Migration and recovery

`AttachmentMigration` runs at startup before normal work:

1. Process semaphore + cross-process file lock.
2. Durable journal under `noBackupFilesDir`, atomically rewritten at every phase.
3. Preflight duplicate paths, root containment, size/SHA-256, and free-space check.
4. Generate/install the wrapped key sidecar.
5. Stream-encrypt to temp, fsync, verify plaintext digest.
6. Authenticate the encrypted temp file and compare against the row.
7. Atomically install ciphertext, update the Room path.
8. Delete plaintext only after verification and row update.
9. Resume interrupted cleanup by re-verifying the ciphertext.
10. Surface explicit `KEY_UNAVAILABLE`, `LOW_STORAGE`, or `RECOVERY_REQUIRED` states. Corrupt/duplicate/out-of-root/orphan cases preserve originals.

A final-review fix now also validates an already-encrypted row’s key sidecar, so a missing key cannot be reported as “Enabled.”

### Backup integration

- Phase 2.2 container is unchanged; inner archive schema is now **2** (schema 1 still restores).
- Encrypted attachment ciphertext and raw Tink keysets are stored **inside** the authenticated `KONNXBK2` container.
- Attachment keys remain independent of backup DEK/KEK, SQLCipher, and other attachments.
- Restore unwraps in memory, generates a fresh local key, re-encrypts for the target owner, verifies size/SHA-256, then imports. No plaintext attachment reaches disk.
- Missing/corrupt attachments are skipped or marked failed without aborting message restore.

**Also present in the working tree:** the previously completed, uncommitted Phase 2.2 backup changes (`BACKUP_ENCRYPTION_PHASE2_2_REPORT.md`, `EncryptedBackupContainer.kt`, `AndroidBackupCredentialStore.kt`, backup settings/storage changes, and related docs/tests). They remain in the same uncommitted change set.

### Remaining limitations

- **Not E2EE and not server-side encryption.** Server-held attachment blobs and the server database remain plaintext.
- **Unlocked-device malware** can read decrypted streams; at-rest encryption only protects the locked/offline file state.
- **Backup attachment packaging is memory-bound**: 256 MiB per attachment entry, 1 GiB archive cap. Storage crypto streams; backup export/restore does not fully.
- **Migration crash recovery is fault-injected** at nine journal checkpoints, not a real OS process kill. Low storage is injected, not a real ENOSPC.
- **No share UI** obtains a URI grant; provider behavior is tested directly, not against a third-party app.
- No thumbnail/image cache exists; a future one must reuse the encrypted path.
- **Emulator-only evidence**; physical phones were untouched. The R8 APK was built, not installed/run.
- No independent cryptographic review.

- HEAD: `1d1b98d`
- `git diff --check`: clean
- Working tree is **dirty** with the Phase 2.3 files above plus the pre-existing Phase 2.2 changes.
- **No commit, tag, push, or branch change was made.**

# Phase 2.4 — Full Storage Security Integration Audit & Hardening

Phase 1 (SQLCipher), Phase 2 (database migration), Phase 2.2 (encrypted backups), and Phase 2.3 (attachment encryption) have been implemented.

Now perform a comprehensive integration security audit across all four phases.

This is a production-readiness exercise, not a documentation-only review.

## 1\. Repository audit

Inspect the actual implementation of:

- SQLCipher database encryption and Keystore key wrapping.
- Database migration journal and recovery.
- Encrypted backup container and credential management.
- Backup restore and attachment re-encryption.
- Attachment streaming encryption and key storage.
- Attachment migration and cleanup.
- Database, backup and attachment workers.
- Startup ordering and database availability gates.
- All storage providers and temporary-file handling.

Compare implementation against documentation. Treat source code and reproducible tests as authoritative.

## 2\. Key-management integration

Verify:

- Database, backup and attachment keys are cryptographically independent.
- Keystore aliases are distinct and correctly scoped.
- AAD binds the correct identifiers and versions.
- Key loss produces typed recovery states.
- Key rotation cannot silently invalidate existing data.
- Backup recovery works independently of the original installation.
- No key material appears in logs, crash reports or persistent plaintext files.
- Temporary key buffers are wiped where practical.

Create and test a complete key lifecycle and dependency matrix.

## 3\. Cross-feature failure testing

Test combinations, not just individual components:

- Database migration interrupted while backup is running.
- Attachment migration interrupted during backup creation.
- Restore interrupted during attachment re-encryption.
- Keystore alias missing while restoring.
- Backup credential rotation during concurrent backup operations.
- Duplicate backup or attachment identifiers.
- Database restored with missing attachment blobs.
- Attachment key sidecar corrupted.
- Low storage during simultaneous migration and restore.
- Process death during every durable state transition.
- Application downgrade followed by upgrade.
- Repeated startup after interrupted recovery.

Use real process termination where feasible and fault injection for deterministic coverage.

Never use destructive testing on physical phones.

## 4\. Concurrency and consistency

Review all locks, transactions and file operations.

Ensure:

- No two migrations operate on the same files concurrently.
- Backup cannot capture partially migrated data.
- Restore cannot overwrite active application data.
- Attachment deletion cannot race with backup reads.
- Database references never point to incomplete files.
- Atomic replacement and cleanup preserve recoverability.
- Workers cannot bypass startup or migration gates.

Add tests for genuine cross-process races where feasible.

## 5\. Data leakage audit

Inspect actual persistent storage, including:

- Database, WAL and SHM.
- Backup temporary files.
- Attachment temporary files.
- Key sidecars.
- Cache and files directories.
- External storage.
- Logs and crash diagnostics.

Search for synthetic plaintext messages, attachment contents, passwords and recovery keys.

Document every remaining plaintext exposure.

## 6\. Performance and resource limits

Measure on API 28 and API 35 emulators:

- Database startup and encrypted query overhead.
- Backup creation and restore time.
- Attachment encryption and decryption throughput.
- Peak memory usage.
- Temporary storage requirements.
- Behavior near configured file and archive limits.

Do not extrapolate emulator results to physical devices.

## 7\. Regression requirements

Run:

- Full JVM test suite.
- Full server test suite.
- Full API 28 instrumentation.
- Full API 35 instrumentation.
- Debug build.
- Release build.
- R8-minified release build.
- Database migration tests.
- Encrypted backup tests.
- Attachment migration tests.

Run affected tests again after every security fix.

## 8\. Findings and remediation

- Critical
- High
- Medium
- Low
- Informational

- Exact source file and location.
- Reproduction procedure.
- Security impact.
- Required remediation.

Fix Critical and High findings before declaring readiness.

Do not hide unresolved findings behind documentation changes.

## Phase 2.4 — Integration audit completed

**Verdict: NOT PRODUCTION READY.** Three High integration defects were fixed and regression-tested. One High product gap remains: **a fresh installation cannot restore an existing backup**, because restore is addressed only by a local `backup_records` row and configured destination. ### Fixes made

1. **Device identity fail-closed**
   - `SecureSecretStore.read()` now distinguishes absent from undecryptable.
   - `DeviceIdentityProvider` throws `DeviceIdentityUnavailableException` instead of generating a new ID.
   - Identity generation is mutex-protected.
   - DataStore is now one instance per file path, preventing multiple providers from corrupting/regenerating identity.
   - Migration maps identity loss to `KEY_UNAVAILABLE`; backup and sync return typed `NotConfigured` failures.

2. **Cross-feature attachment coordination**
   - Added `AttachmentStorageGate`, shared by migration, save, delete, backup export/restore and duplicate cleanup.
   - `AppContainer` wires one gate for all components.
   - Migration keeps its cross-process file lock.

3. **Restore no longer overwrites live data**
   - Existing profile, conversations, messages, contacts and attachment rows are preserved.
   - Old archives are additive only.
   - Duplicate restored attachment files are deleted after the DB transaction.

4. **Security-panel truthfulness**
   - `secretsEncryptedAtRest` now reflects actual Keystore availability instead of always reporting `true`.

## Phase 2.5 — Fresh Install Backup Recovery

Writing

# Phase 2.5 — Fresh Installation Backup Recovery

Phase 2.4 identified F-04 (High): a fresh KONNEX installation cannot restore an existing encrypted backup because restore currently depends on a local backup\_records entry.

Implement a complete, secure backup recovery workflow.

## 1\. Initial audit

Inspect the existing:

- KONNXBK2 encrypted container.
- Argon2id passphrase unlock.
- 160-bit recovery-key mechanism.
- Backup credential storage.
- Restore engine and archive validation.
- Attachment encryption and re-encryption.
- Database availability and migration gates.
- Compose navigation and settings UI.

Preserve the existing backup format unless a justified compatibility change is necessary.

## 2\. Fresh-install recovery

Allow users to restore from:

- A local SAF document URI.
- An externally supplied encrypted backup file.
- A supported configured remote storage destination.

Do not require a pre-existing local backup record.

Support both independent unlock methods:

- Backup passphrase.
- Recovery key.

Never transmit passphrases or recovery keys to the server.

## 3\. Recovery workflow

Implement the following sequence:

1. Select an encrypted backup.
2. Inspect and validate its public header.
3. Identify supported format and version.
4. Request the appropriate unlock credential.
5. Authenticate the container.
6. Validate archive structure and integrity.
7. Display a restore summary.
8. Require explicit user confirmation.
9. Restore database data transactionally.
10. Re-encrypt attachments using fresh local Keystore-backed keys.
11. Verify restored data and attachment references.
12. Report success or a typed recoverable failure.

Never write decrypted archive contents to persistent plaintext storage.

## 4\. Security and recovery behavior

Handle:

- Wrong passphrase.
- Invalid recovery key.
- Corrupt or truncated container.
- Unsupported backup version.
- Missing attachment blobs.
- Missing or invalid Keystore aliases.
- Insufficient storage.
- Process death during restore.
- Repeated restore attempts.
- Existing local data.
- Interrupted database migration.
- Downgrade and upgrade scenarios.

Do not overwrite newer local data silently.

Do not generate replacement device identities when existing identity material is unavailable.

## 5\. Restore safety

Use a staging strategy and persistent recovery journal.

The original application data must remain recoverable until the restored state has been independently verified.

Ensure attachment files and database references cannot become inconsistent.

Do not expose a success state before all required verification completes.

## 6\. UI

Implement a real Compose recovery flow.

Include:

- Select backup.
- Passphrase/recovery-key entry.
- Backup inspection.
- Restore preview.
- Confirmation.
- Progress.
- Recoverable errors.
- Completion summary.

Do not expose credentials in logs, saved state or analytics.

## 7\. Tests

Add JVM and instrumentation tests for:

- Fresh-install restore.
- Both unlock methods.
- Corrupted and truncated containers.
- Invalid credentials.
- Process death at every durable checkpoint.
- Attachment re-encryption.
- Existing data preservation.
- Missing attachments.
- Insufficient storage.
- Repeated restore.
- Real cross-process locking where feasible.

Run full API 28 and API 35 regression suites.

Run debug, release and R8 builds.

## 8\. Documentation

Create:

`BACKUP_RECOVERY_PHASE2_5_REPORT.md`

Update security, backup, key-management, architecture and threat-model documentation.

Phase 2.5 is implemented and verified. Final summary:

## What was built

**Fresh-install recovery engine** (`BackupRecoveryEngine`) that never touches `backup_records`:
- **Sources:** a SAF document the user picks (`ActivityResultContracts.OpenDocument`) or any `.konnexbackup` discovered in a configured, readable destination (`provider.list("konnex-backups")`).
- **Two unlock methods:** passphrase (Argon2id) or the 160-bit recovery key (HKDF) — exactly one, and only the slot the container actually has. Credentials are wiped after every step and never persisted, logged, or sent anywhere.
- **New container primitive:** `EncryptedBackupContainer.checkCredential` unwraps the DEK and wipes it without walking the body, so "wrong passphrase" and "damaged container" become distinct typed outcomes (`WRONG_CREDENTIAL` / `INVALID_RECOVERY_KEY` vs `CORRUPT_CONTAINER`). Format, AADs and limits are unchanged — existing `KONNXBK2` backups stay valid.
- **Flow:** stage → inspect public header → unlock/authenticate → verify every chunk+footer → parse/validate archive → preview (counts, attachment coverage, local conflicts) → explicit confirmation bound to the container's SHA-256 fingerprint → re-authenticate → re-encrypt attachments under fresh local keys → one Room transaction with in-transaction read-back → decrypt every restored attachment again to verify → typed result.
- **Crash safety:** non-secret `restore-journal.json` (fsync + atomic replace, phases `STAGED`/`PREPARED`/`ATTACHMENTS_WRITTEN`/`COMMITTING`/`COMMITTED`/`FAILED`), an OS `FileLock` (`restore.lock`), and `recoverInterrupted()` which deletes `-restored-` files no row references. A crash can leave extra files but never a row pointing at a missing file, and success is never reported before verification.
- **Compose flow:** select → credential entry → preview → progress with typed step labels → completion summary, with typed errors and retry to the failing step. Entry point added to Backup settings so it exists on a new install.

Also fixed a real reporting defect found by the new tests: `RestoreReport.missingAttachments` always reported 0 because it queried the table after inserting the `MISSING` row (Medium, now counted against what the archive actually carried).

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| JVM `test` (distinct) | **122** passed (domain:backup 53, +6 new) |
| Android unit `testDebugUnitTest` | **32** passed |
| Server `-p server test --rerun-tasks` | **35** passed |
| API 35 instrumentation | **99/99** (19 recovery + 32 + 24 + 24) |
| API 28 instrumentation | **99/99** (same groups) |
| debug / release / R8 release builds | all pass |
| `verifyEncryptionStack` | SQLCipher 4.17.0 / Room 2.6.1 / sqlite 2.6.2 / Kotlin 2.1.20 |

Measured recovery (emulators, both running suites concurrently → upper bounds): stage 11.4/24.5 ms; prepare 7 890/17 718 ms; restore 8 479/18 249 ms for a 1.1 KB container on API 28/35 — dominated by Argon2id, not I/O.

* Đã nắm được Phase 2.5. Theo báo cáo hiện tại:

- Phase 2.2: Backup encryption — hoàn thành.
- Phase 2.3: Attachment encryption — hoàn thành.
- Phase 2.4: Integration security audit — hoàn thành, F-04 đã đóng.
- Phase 2.5: Fresh-install backup recovery — hoàn thành.
- F-05: Restore/archive xử lý trong RAM — vẫn chưa giải quyết.
- Independent cryptographic review — chưa thực hiện.
- Chưa commit bất kỳ thay đổi nào.

Bước tiếp theo hợp lý là Phase 2.6: Streaming Backup & Restore.

Mục tiêu chính:

1. Loại bỏ việc materialize toàn bộ archive trong RAM.
2. Streaming export trực tiếp từ Room sang encrypted container.
3. Streaming restore, xác thực từng chunk trước khi import.
4. Giới hạn RAM độc lập với kích thước backup.
5. Journal và phục hồi khi process chết giữa restore.
6. Không làm thay đổi định dạng KONNXBK2 hoặc phá backup cũ.
7. Kiểm thử trên API 28 và API 35, bao gồm dữ liệu lớn và low-storage.

Mình có thể viết prompt tiếng Anh đầy đủ cho Phase 2.6, theo đúng cách làm các phase trước: audit trước, implementation sau, kiểm thử thực tế và báo cáo những gì chưa xác minh.



Dưới đây là prompt tiếng Anh cho Phase 2.6 — Streaming Backup & Restore, tiếp nối trực tiếp Phase 2.5. Prompt đặt trọng tâm vào xử lý dữ liệu lớn, giới hạn RAM, crash recovery và giữ tương thích với KONNXBK2 hiện tại.

# Phase 2.6 — Streaming Backup & Restore (Production Hardening)

Implement Phase 2.6 of KONNEX: eliminate memory-bound backup and restore operations while preserving the existing encryption architecture, backup compatibility, database integrity, and crash-recovery guarantees.

This is a production security and reliability task. Audit the actual repository before making changes. Do not assume that previous phase reports accurately describe the current implementation.

## 1\. Initial Audit — Mandatory

Before implementation, inspect:

- `EncryptedBackupContainer.kt`
- `BackupEngineImpl.kt`
- `BackupRecoveryEngineImpl.kt`
- `BackupArchive.kt`
- `RoomBackupDataSource.kt`
- `RestoreJournal.kt`
- `RestoreProcessLock.kt`
- `LocalAttachmentStore.kt`
- `AttachmentStorageGate.kt`
- All backup storage providers and SAF integration.
- All backup-related Compose screens and state holders.
- Existing migration, database availability, and worker coordination.

Trace every production allocation and I/O path.

Identify:

- Full-container byte arrays.
- Full ZIP/archive materialization.
- Full attachment materialization.
- Temporary plaintext files.
- Unbounded buffers.
- Duplicate copies of encrypted or decrypted data.
- Restore transaction memory usage.
- Existing journal and recovery guarantees.

Write an audit report before implementation.

## 2\. Streaming Encrypted Container

Preserve the existing `KONNXBK2` format and backward compatibility.

Implement streaming APIs for:

- Container creation.
- Container authentication.
- Container decryption.
- Credential verification.
- Container inspection.
- Passphrase rewrapping.
- Recovery-key rewrapping.

Requirements:

- AES-256-GCM.
- Existing 64 KiB chunk format.
- Existing authenticated header, chunk ordering, final markers, and footer.
- Existing Argon2id parameters and HKDF-SHA256 derivation.
- Unique nonces.
- Strict chunk ordering and truncation detection.
- Authenticate every chunk and footer before considering the container valid.
- Never expose unauthenticated plaintext to archive parsing or database import.
- Preserve existing recovery and passphrase behavior.

Do not silently introduce a new backup format.

If a format change is unavoidable, introduce an explicit new version and retain readers for existing backups.

## 3\. Streaming Backup Export

Replace the current in-memory export pipeline.

Required pipeline:

`Room data → bounded archive writer → streaming encrypted container → temporary encrypted file → verified destination`

Requirements:

- Stream attachment content instead of materializing attachment byte arrays.
- Use bounded buffers.
- Avoid writing plaintext archive files to persistent storage.
- Do not retain the complete ZIP or container in memory.
- Verify encrypted output before publishing it.
- Use temporary encrypted files and atomic publication where supported.
- Clean up incomplete files after errors.
- Preserve existing backup metadata and attachment integrity.
- Do not delete an existing valid backup if a new backup fails.

Define and enforce explicit maximum limits for:

- Individual attachment size.
- Total archive size.
- Number of archive entries.
- Entry names and metadata lengths.
- Total decompressed size.

Prevent ZIP bombs, duplicate entries, path traversal, and integer overflow.

## 4\. Streaming Restore

Replace the current memory-bound restore path.

Required pipeline:

`Selected source → encrypted container verification → authenticated streaming decryption → bounded archive parser → staging → verified Room import`

Requirements:

- Do not parse archive data before container authentication.
- Do not materialize the entire encrypted container or decrypted archive.
- Stream attachments directly into encrypted staging files.
- Never write attachment plaintext to persistent storage.
- Re-encrypt restored attachments using fresh local Tink keysets and the target device's Keystore.
- Preserve owner-bound AAD.
- Validate attachment size and SHA-256.
- Validate schema version, entity counts, IDs, relationships, and foreign keys.
- Prevent duplicate IDs and path collisions.
- Preserve additive restore semantics.
- Never roll back newer local records.

### Database consistency

Do not claim that a streaming implementation is atomic merely because it uses a Room transaction.

Evaluate whether large imports can safely fit into one transaction.

If necessary, implement a durable staged-import design with:

- Import identifier.
- Staging metadata.
- Durable journal.
- Explicit commit state.
- Recovery after process death.
- Cleanup of unreferenced staged files.
- Verification that no database row references a missing attachment.

A partially completed restore must never be reported as successful.

## 5\. Crash Recovery and Storage Safety

Extend the existing restore journal rather than creating an unrelated recovery mechanism.

Test process interruption at:

- Source staging.
- Header inspection.
- Credential verification.
- Chunk authentication.
- Archive parsing.
- Attachment staging.
- Database import.
- Database commit.
- Attachment publication.
- Final cleanup.

Requirements:

- Use atomic journal updates with fsync.
- Use cross-process file locking.
- Coordinate with `AttachmentStorageGate`.
- Preserve existing backup files.
- Never delete the only valid copy.
- Detect insufficient storage before destructive operations.
- Recover deterministically after process death and reboot.
- Distinguish retryable failures from permanent corruption.

Test real process termination where possible, not only simulated exceptions.

## 6\. Storage Provider Compatibility

Audit and test:

- Local device storage.
- Android SAF document providers.
- WebDAV.
- Google Drive.
- KONNEX server storage.

Do not claim streaming support for a provider unless its actual adapter supports streaming.

If a provider cannot support reliable streaming or atomic publication, document its limitations and fail safely.

Avoid loading remote files completely into memory.

## 7\. Performance and Memory Tests

Test with synthetic backup datasets of:

- 10 MB.
- 100 MB.
- 500 MB.
- 1 GB, if emulator storage permits.

Measure:

- Peak Java heap.
- Peak native memory where measurable.
- Export duration.
- Restore duration.
- Encryption/decryption throughput.
- Temporary disk usage.
- Number of buffer allocations.
- Garbage collection frequency.
- Recovery time after interruption.

The critical requirement is that peak application memory must remain bounded and must not grow linearly with total backup size.

Set an explicit memory budget and justify it with measurements.

Do not extrapolate emulator performance to physical devices.

## 8\. Security Requirements

Verify:

- No passphrase in files, preferences, logs, or saved state.
- No recovery key in plaintext persistent storage.
- No plaintext backup archive on disk.
- No plaintext attachment staging.
- No raw DEK persistence.
- No unauthenticated plaintext exposed to consumers.
- No downgrade to plaintext backup format.
- No silent fallback when authentication fails.
- No deletion of valid backups during failed export or restore.
- Temporary buffers containing secrets are wiped where practical.

Do not invent cryptographic primitives.

Prefer established libraries and existing tested implementations.

Document any unavoidable secret lifetime in memory.

## 9\. Testing Requirements

Add tests for:

### JVM tests

- Streaming encryption/decryption.
- Existing KONNXBK2 compatibility.
- Chunk ordering and authentication.
- Truncation and tampering.
- Wrong credentials.
- ZIP traversal and ZIP bomb protection.
- Duplicate entries.
- Integer overflow.
- Large attachments.
- Bounded-memory behavior.
- Interrupted export.
- Interrupted restore.
- Journal recovery.
- Repeated restore.
- Concurrent backup and restore.
- Concurrent attachment migration.

### Android instrumentation

Run on API 28 and API 35:

- Real Keystore-backed recovery key.
- Real SQLCipher database.
- Real Room import.
- Real encrypted attachment storage.
- SAF provider tests where feasible.
- Process-death recovery.
- Insufficient-storage behavior.
- R8/minified release compatibility.

Run existing regression suites as well.

## 10\. Regression and Compatibility

Do not break:

- SQLCipher 4.17.0.
- Room 2.6.1.
- Existing KONNXBK2 backups.
- Passphrase recovery.
- Recovery-key recovery.
- Phase 2.3 attachment encryption.
- Phase 2.4 storage coordination.
- Phase 2.5 fresh-install recovery.
- Existing additive restore behavior.
- Existing downgrade protections.

Do not modify E2EE protocols, server authentication, database schemas, or unrelated application features unless strictly necessary.

## 11\. Documentation

Create:

`BACKUP_STREAMING_PHASE2_6_REPORT.md`

Update relevant:

- `SECURITY.md`
- `BACKUP.md`
- `ENCRYPTED_BACKUP.md`
- `KEY_MANAGEMENT.md`
- `THREAT_MODEL.md`
- `ARCHITECTURE.md`
- `README.md`

Document:

- Streaming architecture.
- Buffer and memory limits.
- Temporary-file behavior.
- Authentication guarantees.
- Crash recovery.
- Provider limitations.
- Performance measurements.
- Compatibility.
- Remaining risks.

## 12\. Git and Device Safety

- Do not commit.
- Do not push.
- Do not create tags.
- Do not change branches.
- Do not install anything on physical phones.
- Use disposable API 28 and API 35 emulators only.
- Preserve the existing working tree and unrelated uncommitted changes.
- Do not reset, clean, or overwrite existing user data.
- Keep temporary probes under `.logs/` if that directory is git-ignored.

## 13\. Final Acceptance Criteria

Phase 2.6 can only be marked complete if:

1. Backup export no longer materializes the full archive in memory.
2. Restore no longer materializes the full container or archive in memory.
3. Attachment processing is streaming.
4. Peak memory remains within a documented fixed budget.
5. Existing KONNXBK2 backups remain readable.
6. Authentication occurs before archive data is consumed.
7. Crash recovery works at the critical commit boundaries.
8. No plaintext backup or attachment staging is found.
9. API 28 and API 35 regression suites pass.
10. Debug, release, and R8 builds pass.
11. Existing security guarantees remain intact.

If any criterion cannot be verified, explicitly mark it BLOCKED or NOT VERIFIED.

Do not report simulated tests as real process-death tests.

Do not claim a provider supports streaming without testing its actual implementation.

Do not claim production readiness merely because compilation succeeds.

**Start with the repository audit. Then implement incrementally, run tests after each major component, and finish with an evidence-based report.**

Lưu ý: Phase 2.6 nên được xem là một phase hardening lớn, không phải chỉ tối ưu `ByteArray`. Điểm quan trọng nhất là bảo đảm dữ liệu chưa xác thực không bao giờ được đưa vào parser hoặc Room, đồng thời crash giữa lúc import không làm database và attachment files mất đồng bộ.

Phase 2.6 is implemented, verified, and reported. Here is the evidence-based summary.

## What was built

**Streaming container** (`EncryptedBackupContainer.kt`) — `openWriter`/`StreamEncryptor` and `openReader`/`StreamDecryptor` (`readHeader`, `verifyBody`, `openPlaintext`, `read`, `finish`), all over the **unchanged KONNXBK2 byte layout** (AES-256-GCM, 64 KiB chunks, index-bound AAD, final marker, authenticated footer, same Argon2id/HKDF parameters). `verifyBody()` authenticates every chunk and the footer with the DEK before a single plaintext byte is exposed, then `openPlaintext()` re-reads with the same DEK — one Argon2 per restore. `inspect`, `verifyCredential`, both rewrap paths are streaming too; the legacy `encrypt()` now delegates, so there is one implementation of the format.

**Streaming export/restore** — new `BackupArchiveStream` writer/reader (bounded buffers, digest-per-entry, order-agnostic reading for old backups), `exportBackupFiles`/`restoreBackupFiles` in `LocalAttachmentStore`, `BackupEngineImpl` (temp encrypted file → fsync → verify → atomic publish; restore: verify → decrypt → parse → ciphertext-only staging → fresh Tink keyset re-encrypt → one chunked Room transaction), `BackupRecoveryEngineImpl` streaming `authenticate`, and WebDAV file-streaming upload/download.

**Limits** — 256 MiB/entry, 32 MiB payload, 1 MiB manifest, 64 KiB key envelope, 1 GiB total decompressed, 10 000 entries, 255-char names, `Math.addExact` throughout; traversal, duplicates, unmanifested entries and bombs rejected.

## Bugs found and fixed during verification

1. `StreamDecryptor.restart()` didn't reset `footerVerified` → every verify-then-decrypt flow failed.
2. Trailing bytes after the footer were accepted.
3. `ZipOutputStream` was being double-wrapped (nested ZIP) in the engine and instrumentation tests.
4. `BackupArchiveStreamReader` closed the plaintext stream it was handed, so the container could never be finalised — ownership now stays with the caller.

## Verification (actual results)

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| JVM `./gradlew test` | **172 tests, 0 failures** (`domain:backup` 53 → **68**, incl. 15 new streaming tests) |
| API 35 `emulator-5554` | **102 instrumentation tests, 0 failures** (stream 3, fresh 19, g1 32, g2 24, g3 24) |
| API 28 `emulator-5556` | **102 instrumentation tests, 0 failures** (same groups) |
| Real process death | `shortMsg=Process crashed.` during attachment staging; new process recovered → `OK (1 test)`, only `restore.lock` left |
| Builds | debug, debugAndroidTest, release, `-PkonnexMinifyRelease=true` (R8) all **SUCCESSFUL** |

**Memory/throughput (48 MiB attachment, measured, not extrapolated):** container 50.56 MB (0.45 % overhead); export 11.6 s / restore 23.8 s on API 35; **retained heap after GC 0 B** for restore on both APIs and 0 B / 1.47 MiB for export — constant, not proportional to payload. Budget: ≤ 32 MiB retained, ≤ 176 MiB allocation pressure.

## Honest gaps (in the report as NOT VERIFIED / PARTIAL)

100 MB/500 MB/1 GB datasets not run; native memory not measured; WebDAV streaming only mock-tested (no live server); SAF/Drive streaming partial; kill *inside* the Room import not separately tested (SQLite rollback relied on); disk-full path untested; R8 APK built but not instrumented; emulator numbers don't extrapolate to phones.

## Artifacts and Git

- `BACKUP_STREAMING_PHASE2_6_AUDIT.md` (written before implementation) and `BACKUP_STREAMING_PHASE2_6_REPORT.md`.
- Docs updated: SECURITY, BACKUP, ENCRYPTED_BACKUP, KEY_MANAGEMENT, THREAT_MODEL, ARCHITECTURE, README.
- HEAD still `1d1b98d`; **nothing committed, pushed, tagged or branched**; physical phones untouched; probes under `.logs/`; `git diff --check` clean.

Verdict in the report: streaming, bounded memory, authenticate-before-parse, format compatibility and crash recovery are verified; **production readiness is not declared** until the large-dataset, native-memory and drive/SAF items above are closed.

## 2. Đánh giá kết quả Phase 2.6

Mình đánh giá đây là một bước tiến quan trọng vì đã giải quyết được nhiều vấn đề nền tảng:

- Không còn phải nạp toàn bộ archive vào RAM.
- Giữ nguyên định dạng backup KONNXBK2.
- Xác thực container trước khi đưa dữ liệu vào archive parser.
- Streaming attachment.
- Có crash recovery bằng journal.
- Có thử nghiệm SIGKILL thật và khôi phục bằng process mới.
- Có kiểm thử API 28 và API 35.

Đặc biệt, thử nghiệm SIGKILL là bằng chứng có giá trị hơn nhiều so với việc chỉ giả lập exception.

Tuy nhiên, mình muốn lưu ý một chi tiết kỹ thuật: báo cáo ghi nhận `verifyBody()` xác thực toàn bộ container trước rồi mới mở luồng plaintext và đọc lại. Cách này tăng độ an toàn nhưng cần tiếp tục kiểm tra việc nguồn dữ liệu có bị thay đổi giữa hai lần đọc hay không. Nên xác thực fingerprint hoặc bảo đảm nguồn bất biến trong toàn bộ quá trình.

## 5\. Một quyết định kiến trúc rất quan trọng

Mình muốn KONNEX giữ vững triết lý:

Local-first – Privacy-first – Storage Anywhere – User-owned Data.

Điều đó có nghĩa:

- Server không sở hữu dữ liệu của người dùng.
- Người dùng kiểm soát nơi lưu dữ liệu.
- Mất server không đồng nghĩa mất tin nhắn đã lưu.
- Đổi điện thoại vẫn có thể phục hồi dữ liệu.
- Không phụ thuộc duy nhất vào Google Drive hoặc một nhà cung cấp cloud.
- Bảo mật không được đánh đổi để lấy sự tiện lợi.

Đây nên là nguyên tắc xuyên suốt tất cả các Phase tiếp theo.

# 19 findings

Theo báo cáo audit nội bộ

- Không có lỗi CRITICAL.
- Không có lỗi HIGH có thể khai thác trong crypto và backup/restore.
- Một lỗi HIGH liên quan đến danh tính ký bản Release.
- Hai vấn đề bảo mật Android đã được sửa và kiểm thử.
- Chưa có đánh giá mật mã độc lập bên ngoài.

### Những điểm đã làm tốt

- `FLAG_SECURE` được bật để hạn chế chụp màn hình các nội dung nhạy cảm.
- Android backup và device-to-device extraction được cấu hình rõ ràng.
- Đã kiểm thử trên API 28 và API 35.
- Không thay đổi thuật toán, tham số mật mã hoặc định dạng KONNXBK2.
- Giữ nguyên các thay đổi chưa commit của những Phase trước.

## 2. Ba vấn đề cần lưu ý trước khi phát hành

| Vấn đề | Mức độ | Hướng xử lý |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Release dùng debug keystore | HIGH | Tạo signing identity chính thức |
| Dependency verification/locking | MEDIUM | Bổ sung kiểm soát chuỗi cung ứng |
| Immutable String chứa credentials | MEDIUM | Thiết kế giảm thời gian tồn tại secret |

Đặc biệt, lỗi ký APK bằng debug keystore phải được giải quyết trước khi phát hành thương mại.

## 3. Roadmap hiện tại

1. Phase 2.2 – 2.6
   Hoàn thành theo báo cáoBackup, encryption, recovery, streaming.
2. Phase 2.8 – Security Review
   Hoàn thành có giới hạn19 findings, 2 hardening fixes.
3. Phase 3 – E2EE
   Bắt đầu tiếp theoThiết kế và triển khai mã hóa đầu cuối.
4. Phase 4 – Messaging Infrastructure
   Server, message delivery, synchronization.
5. Phase 5 – Voice & Video
   Audio, video, WebRTC.
6. Phase 6 – Cross-platform
   iOS, Windows, Linux.
7. Phase 7 – Production Readiness
   Hoàn thiện bảo mật, ký ứng dụng, kiểm thử và phát hành.

## 4\. Bước tiếp theo: Phase 3 – E2EE

Mình khuyên không nên yêu cầu OpenCode viết ngay chức năng mã hóa tin nhắn.

Nên chia Phase 3 thành các bước:

| Phase | Nội dung |
| --- | --- |
| 3.0 | E2EE Architecture & Protocol Design |
| 3.1 | Device Identity & Key Management |
| 3.2 | One-to-One Encrypted Messaging |
| 3.3 | Multi-Device Encryption |
| 3.4 | Group Encryption |
| 3.5 | Key Rotation & Device Revocation |
| 3.6 | E2EE Security Testing |

Điểm đặc biệt quan trọng: KONNEX cần hỗ trợ nhiều thiết bị nhưng vẫn giữ nguyên quyền kiểm soát khóa của người dùng. Backup recovery không được vô tình trở thành cơ chế khiến server có thể đọc tin nhắn.

Mình đề xuất bắt đầu bằng Phase 3.0, chỉ thiết kế giao thức và threat model, chưa viết implementation. Sau khi thiết kế được kiểm tra, mới cho OpenCode triển khai từng phần.

Như vậy, chúng ta sẽ tránh phải thay đổi toàn bộ hệ thống mã hóa khi đã có nhiều người dùng và dữ liệu thật.



Tiếp theo là Phase 3.0 – E2EE Architecture & Protocol Design.

Mục tiêu của Phase này là thiết kế hoàn chỉnh nền tảng mã hóa đầu cuối cho KONNEX trước khi viết code. Đặc biệt phải giải quyết bài toán nhiều thiết bị, chat nhóm, backup, phục hồi tài khoản và quản lý khóa.

Mình chọn hướng sử dụng các giao thức mật mã đã được kiểm chứng, không tự sáng chế giao thức mới.

Định hướng Phase 3.0

- Chưa triển khai E2EE production code.
- Chưa thay đổi database schema.
- Chưa thay đổi backup format KONNXBK2.
- Chưa thay đổi server authentication.
- Thiết kế tương thích Android hiện tại và mở rộng đa nền tảng.
- Có threat model và protocol specification trước khi triển khai.

## Prompt dành cho OpenCode

Writing

# Phase 3.0 — KONNEX End-to-End Encryption Architecture & Protocol Design

Design the complete End-to-End Encryption (E2EE) architecture and protocol specification for KONNEX.

This phase establishes the cryptographic foundation for all future messaging features.

**IMPORTANT: This is a DESIGN-ONLY phase. Do not implement the production E2EE protocol yet.**

The existing KONNEX backup, encryption, storage, recovery, and Android architecture must remain intact.

The objective is to produce a technically rigorous, implementation-ready E2EE specification that can be independently reviewed before Phase 3.1 begins.

---

# 1\. Initial Repository Audit

Before designing anything, inspect the actual repository.

Review:

- Current Android application architecture.
- Domain and data modules.
- Account and authentication models.
- Device identity and installation identity.
- Existing database entities.
- Message-related models and interfaces.
- Attachment encryption.
- Android Keystore integration.
- Tink usage.
- Backup and recovery architecture.
- Multi-device assumptions.
- Server communication architecture.
- Existing networking and API contracts.
- Dependency catalog and cryptographic libraries.

Inspect all existing security documentation.

Do not assume previous phase reports accurately describe the current implementation.

Create an architecture inventory describing:

- Existing functionality.
- Missing functionality.
- Reusable components.
- Components that must remain isolated.
- Compatibility constraints.

Do not modify existing production code during the initial audit.

# 2\. E2EE Design Principles

KONNEX E2EE must follow these principles:

1. Privacy-first.
2. Zero plaintext message access for the server.
3. User-controlled cryptographic identities.
4. Independent device identities.
5. Multi-device support.
6. Secure group messaging.
7. Forward secrecy.
8. Post-compromise security where supported by the selected protocol.
9. Secure device revocation.
10. Safe key rotation.
11. Offline message delivery.
12. Cross-platform interoperability.
13. No dependence on Google Drive or any single cloud provider.
14. No custom cryptographic primitives.

The server must operate as an untrusted message relay.

The server may store encrypted message envelopes and routing metadata, but must not possess the keys required to decrypt message content.

Document unavoidable metadata leakage explicitly.

# 3\. Cryptographic Protocol Selection

Evaluate established protocols and implementations.

At minimum, investigate:

- Signal Protocol.
- X3DH and PQXDH.
- Double Ratchet.
- Sesame multi-device session management.
- MLS (Messaging Layer Security, RFC 9420).
- Established group encryption approaches.

Evaluate the suitability of:

- One-to-one conversations.
- Multi-device conversations.
- Group messaging.
- Offline delivery.
- Device revocation.
- Key rotation.
- Forward secrecy.
- Post-compromise security.
- Cross-platform support.
- Long-term maintenance.
- Licensing.
- Available audited implementations.

Do not assume every protocol is interchangeable.

Do not combine Signal and MLS primitives arbitrarily.

Select a coherent architecture with clearly defined protocol boundaries.

Provide a comparison matrix and justify the final recommendation.

Prefer maintained, established implementations over custom cryptographic code.

If a selected library has platform, licensing, or maintenance limitations, document them.

# 4\. Identity and Device Architecture

Design the complete identity model.

Distinguish:

- User account identity.
- User cryptographic identity.
- Device identity.
- Device installation identity.
- Device signing key.
- Device encryption key.
- Session keys.
- Message encryption keys.
- Group epoch keys.

Each device must have an independently verifiable cryptographic identity.

Design:

- Device registration.
- Device linking.
- Device verification.
- Device removal.
- Lost-device recovery.
- Key rotation.
- Device replacement.
- Device migration.

Define how users verify that a newly linked device actually belongs to the intended account.

The server must not be able to silently substitute device public keys.

Evaluate key transparency or equivalent mechanisms for detecting malicious key substitution.

Do not silently regenerate an existing identity after key loss.

# 5\. One-to-One Messaging Protocol

Design the full lifecycle:

1. User identity creation.
2. Device identity creation.
3. Public-key publication.
4. Prekey management.
5. Initial session establishment.
6. Message encryption.
7. Server relay.
8. Offline message storage.
9. Recipient decryption.
10. Message acknowledgement.
11. Session advancement.
12. Key rotation.
13. Device removal.

Specify:

- Message envelope structure.
- Cryptographic associated data.
- Nonce requirements.
- Message identifiers.
- Replay protection.
- Duplicate detection.
- Out-of-order delivery.
- Missing-message recovery.
- Session reset behavior.
- Authentication failure behavior.

The server must never receive plaintext messages or plaintext attachment content.

# 6\. Multi-Device Architecture

A user may own:

- Android phone.
- Android tablet.
- iPhone.
- Windows computer.
- Linux computer.

Each device must have its own cryptographic identity and sessions.

Design:

- Device directory.
- Device trust state.
- Device-to-device synchronization.
- New-device linking.
- Encrypted message fan-out.
- Device revocation.
- Offline devices.
- Removed devices receiving delayed messages.
- Device key replacement.

Do not use one shared private key copied across all devices.

Explain how historical messages become available on a newly linked device without weakening the security of existing sessions.

Distinguish:

- Message synchronization.
- Cryptographic session synchronization.
- Historical message transfer.
- Backup restoration.

# 7\. Group Messaging

Evaluate a suitable group encryption architecture.

Prefer MLS if its implementation and integration are appropriate.

Specify:

- Group creation.
- Member addition.
- Member removal.
- Membership changes.
- Epoch transitions.
- Group key management.
- Message ordering.
- Out-of-order delivery.
- Offline members.
- Group state recovery.
- Group administrator roles.
- Malicious member behavior.

Explain how a removed member loses access to future group messages.

Specify how the architecture prevents a newly added member from automatically gaining access to historical messages unless explicitly authorized.

Do not implement group encryption in this phase.

# 8\. Attachment Encryption

Design how E2EE integrates with existing attachment encryption.

Preserve the existing local attachment storage architecture.

Define:

- Per-attachment encryption keys.
- Attachment key distribution.
- Content authentication.
- Chunked media encryption.
- Attachment integrity verification.
- Thumbnail encryption.
- Attachment sharing in groups.
- Expiration and deletion.
- Key rotation.

Do not expose plaintext attachments to the server.

Do not assume the existing backup attachment encryption automatically provides E2EE.

Clearly distinguish:

- Local-at-rest encryption.
- Backup encryption.
- End-to-end attachment encryption.

# 9\. Backup and Recovery Integration

This is a critical requirement.

Design how E2EE identities and sessions interact with the existing KONNEX backup system.

Preserve:

- KONNXBK2 compatibility.
- Existing backup passphrase.
- Existing recovery key.
- Fresh-install restore.
- Streaming backup and restore.
- Android Keystore protections.

Evaluate:

- Whether identity private keys should be included in encrypted backups.
- How device-bound keys are restored on a new device.
- How to distinguish device identity recovery from account identity recovery.
- Whether historical session state should be restored.
- How to prevent backup rollback attacks.
- How to prevent stolen backups from enabling impersonation.
- How to handle lost recovery credentials.

Never allow the server to decrypt backups or recover E2EE private keys.

Do not automatically restore an old device identity onto a different device.

A restored device must undergo an explicit identity and trust procedure.

Do not modify the existing backup format in this phase.

# 10\. Server Architecture and Trust Boundaries

Design the minimum server responsibilities.

Evaluate:

- Account authentication.
- Device directory.
- Public-key distribution.
- Prekey publication.
- Prekey consumption.
- Encrypted message queues.
- Message acknowledgement.
- Device synchronization.
- Group membership coordination.
- Rate limiting.
- Abuse prevention.

Define what the server can and cannot see.

Explicitly document:

- Sender metadata.
- Recipient metadata.
- Timestamps.
- Message sizes.
- Device relationships.
- Group membership.
- Delivery status.
- IP addresses.

Do not promise perfect metadata privacy unless the architecture actually provides it.

Design defenses against:

- Public-key substitution.
- Replay.
- Duplicate prekey consumption.
- Malicious message ordering.
- Message injection.
- Device impersonation.
- Server rollback.
- Malicious server responses.

# 11\. Cross-Platform Compatibility

The protocol must be platform-independent.

Design a shared protocol layer that can eventually support:

- Android.
- iOS.
- Windows.
- Linux.

Specify:

- Canonical serialization.
- Integer and byte ordering.
- Message versioning.
- Cryptographic algorithm identifiers.
- Protocol version negotiation.
- Compatibility rules.
- Unknown-field behavior.
- Cross-platform test vectors.

Avoid platform-specific serialization of cryptographic messages.

Android Keystore may protect Android-specific private keys, but protocol messages must remain interoperable.

# 12\. Security and Threat Model

Create a detailed E2EE threat model.

Consider:

- Compromised server.
- Malicious server operator.
- Stolen device.
- Rooted Android device.
- Malware on a device.
- Malicious linked device.
- Compromised backup.
- Lost recovery key.
- Public-key substitution.
- Replay attacks.
- Session rollback.
- Malicious group member.
- Removed group member.
- Compromised cryptographic dependency.
- Malicious application update.

For each threat, document:

- Attacker capability.
- Security objective.
- Attack scenario.
- Mitigation.
- Residual risk.
- Verification strategy.

Distinguish threats that can be mitigated from those that remain outside the E2EE security boundary.

# 13\. Protocol State Machines

Provide formal state-machine specifications for:

- User identity.
- Device identity.
- Device trust.
- One-to-one sessions.
- Multi-device sessions.
- Group membership.
- Group epochs.
- Prekey lifecycle.
- Device revocation.
- Recovery.

Document valid transitions and forbidden transitions.

Include failure and recovery paths.

# 14\. Required Deliverables

Create:

1. `E2EE_ARCHITECTURE_PHASE3_0.md`
2. `E2EE_PROTOCOL_SPEC_PHASE3_0.md`
3. `E2EE_THREAT_MODEL_PHASE3_0.md`
4. `E2EE_PROTOCOL_COMPARISON_PHASE3_0.md`
5. `E2EE_KEY_MANAGEMENT_PHASE3_0.md`
6. `E2EE_MULTI_DEVICE_PHASE3_0.md`
7. `E2EE_GROUP_MESSAGING_PHASE3_0.md`
8. `E2EE_BACKUP_RECOVERY_INTEGRATION_PHASE3_0.md`
9. `E2EE_PHASE3_0_REPORT.md`

Include architecture diagrams, message flow diagrams, key lifecycle diagrams, state machines, and protocol decision records.

# 15\. Implementation Readiness Review

Before declaring Phase 3.0 complete, verify that the documentation answers:

- Which exact protocol will be used?
- Which maintained implementation will be used?
- How are user and device identities separated?
- How are multiple devices trusted?
- How are one-to-one sessions established?
- How are group sessions managed?
- How are attachments encrypted?
- How does backup interact with private keys?
- How does device revocation work?
- What metadata remains visible to the server?
- How will Android and future platforms interoperate?
- What happens when a device loses its keys?

Every important design decision must have a documented justification.

List unresolved decisions explicitly.

# 16\. Strict Execution Constraints

This is a design-only phase.

- Do not implement E2EE production code.
- Do not modify existing cryptographic code.
- Do not modify database schemas.
- Do not modify backup format.
- Do not modify server authentication.
- Do not change dependencies.
- Do not introduce new cryptographic libraries.
- Do not modify unrelated application features.
- Do not commit.
- Do not push.
- Do not change branches.
- Do not reset or clean the working tree.
- Preserve all existing uncommitted work.

You may create documentation and diagrams only.

If a design decision cannot be established confidently, document the alternatives and the evidence needed to resolve it.

Do not invent test results or claim an external protocol review has occurred.

# 17\. Final Report

At completion, provide:

- Selected protocol architecture.
- Protocol comparison.
- Key hierarchy.
- Device identity model.
- Multi-device design.
- Group messaging design.
- Backup integration design.
- Server trust boundaries.
- Remaining security questions.
- Exact files created.
- Final Git status.
- Explicit confirmation that no production implementation was modified.

Phase 3.0 is complete only when the design is coherent, implementation-ready, internally consistent, and suitable for independent security review.

**Begin with the repository audit. Design first. Implement only in subsequent phases.**

## Tiêu chí mình sẽ dùng để đánh giá kết quả

Khi OpenCode hoàn thành, bạn gửi lại báo cáo cho mình. Mình sẽ kiểm tra kỹ 5 vấn đề quan trọng nhất:

1. Có chọn giao thức E2EE thực sự phù hợp hay không.
2. Một tài khoản có nhiều thiết bị mà không chia sẻ private key nguy hiểm.
3. Server không thể giả mạo danh tính thiết bị.
4. Backup và khôi phục không làm mất tính bảo mật E2EE.
5. Android hiện tại có thể mở rộng sang iOS, Windows và Linux mà không phải viết lại giao thức.

Lưu ý: Phase 3.0 chỉ thiết kế. Chưa cho OpenCode viết code E2EE. Đây là bước giúp tránh sai kiến trúc ngay từ đầu.



* done:
I'll start with the repository audit — reading the actual current implementation before writing any design.

Now reading the existing models, contracts, server API, and the pre-existing E2EE documents.

Audit complete: 34 Gradle modules, Room v1/SQLCipher, server schema v2 with plaintext `messages.body`, no device/prekey/envelope tables anywhere, and the existing E2EE docs are a pre-Phase-3 sketch. Writing the nine design documents now.

























Phase 3.0 is complete: nine design documents, no code touched, `HEAD` unchanged at `1d1b98d`.

## Verdict: design complete — ready for independent review, **not yet implementation-ready**

Two non-documentation blockers remain: **U-01** (libsignal is AGPL-3.0-only and KONNEX declares no licence) and **U-02** (OpenMLS must ship as a pinned, patched ≥ 0.9.2 through UniFFI on Android). Neither can be closed by writing more specs.

## Selected architecture

| Conversation | Cryptosystem | Implementation | Guarantees |
|---|---|---|---|
| Direct | PQXDH (X25519 + Kyber1024) → Double Ratchet, one session per **recipient device** | libsignal (AGPL gate) | per-message forward secrecy, DH-ratchet PCS, PQ handshake |
| Group | MLS RFC 9420, ciphersuite `0x0001`, one leaf per device | OpenMLS (MIT) via UniFFI | 1 ciphertext/message, epoch FS/PCS, **cryptographic** removal, no PQ |

One `KONNXEE1` envelope (116-byte big-endian header + opaque library ciphertext), deterministic CBOR payload with CDDL, header bound *inside* the authenticated payload because libsignal has no caller AAD. Boundary rule: one suite per conversation (`bindingId`); MLS Welcome/Commit travel in envelopes but no key or ratchet output ever crosses between protocols.

## Key findings from the audit

- 34 modules, `minSdk 26`, server schema v2 — and **no** device table, prekey table, envelope table, device key, or user cryptographic identity anywhere. Sync is account-scoped, so every device sees the same plaintext. Device identity today is only a local `dev-<uuid>` used for attachment owner AAD.
- The pre-existing `E2EE_ARCHITECTURE.md` is a sketch (single device, groups deferred); it is now superseded by the Phase 3.0 set while staying untouched so it keeps describing history honestly.
- Concrete compatibility trap found: Phase 2.6's archive reader **rejects unknown entry prefixes**, so backup archive schema 3 needs a tolerance change *before* any build writes E2EE metadata (U-11).
- Concrete gap found: account sessions aren't bound to `deviceUid`, so a revoked device keeps a valid bearer token (U-14b).

## Key design decisions (14 records, PDR-001…014)

No user-level private key exists — identity is an append-only signed **UIR hash chain** with pinned `(seq, entryHash)`; a lower seq is `E_ROLLBACK`, an equal seq with a different hash is `E_FORK`. Device linking transfers an **authorisation, never a private key** (QR + SAS). Own-device copies are mandatory. Backups contain **history only** — no keys, no sessions, no trust pins, so restore is always a new device. Metadata is documented, never promised away (no sealed sender/padding in v1).

## Remaining security questions

Seven for the reviewer (hdrHash binding vs outer AEAD; 3-epoch retention; chain-only transparency vs global log; `DIK_sig` reuse as MLS leaf key; STREAM framing vs Tink keyset on the wire; whether `HISTORY_SHARE` should exist in v1; whether U-14b must close before any encrypted message ships), plus a 19-item unresolved register `U-01…U-19` with the evidence each needs.

## Files created (9, all new documentation, 3 813 lines)

`E2EE_ARCHITECTURE_PHASE3_0.md` · `E2EE_PROTOCOL_SPEC_PHASE3_0.md` · `E2EE_THREAT_MODEL_PHASE3_0.md` (18 threats) · `E2EE_PROTOCOL_COMPARISON_PHASE3_0.md` · `E2EE_KEY_MANAGEMENT_PHASE3_0.md` · `E2EE_MULTI_DEVICE_PHASE3_0.md` · `E2EE_GROUP_MESSAGING_PHASE3_0.md` · `E2EE_BACKUP_RECOVERY_INTEGRATION_PHASE3_0.md` · `E2EE_PHASE3_0_REPORT.md`

## Verification actually run

`git rev-parse` → `1d1b98d` unchanged; `git status --short` → 79 entries (70 pre-existing Phase 2.2–2.8 entries, byte-identical set, + these 9 docs); every new file exists and is non-empty; the unresolved-ID register and all cross-document references resolve. **No production file was modified** — no Kotlin, Gradle, manifest, schema, `KONNXBK2`, or dependency change; nothing committed, pushed, branched, reset or cleaned.

**Not done, not claimed:** no implementation, no test vectors, no interop run, no benchmark, no external review (the SRLabs/OpenMLS audit I cite is an audit of that library, not of KONNEX). Every mitigation in the threat model is a requirement, not an observed property, and the product statement stays: *"Messages are not end-to-end encrypted yet."*
